I have the feeling that in this comment section, some viewers haven't watched Prof. Jiang's YouTube video till the end. There is confusion, especially for new viewers:

In the video, he wants to explain why the Jewish people are so creative in so many fields: Albert Einstein in physics, Sigmund Freud in psychology, and Karl Marx in sociology. None of the 3 lived in modern Israel. This is not about modern Israel, Zionism, or geopolitics.

This is about the religious innovation of the Old Testament 2500 years ago, in competition with the Greek and Egyptian religions. Back then, there were no Christians or Muslims. Also, many viewpoints of Prof. Jiang, especially the psychological interpretation of King David, would be controversial among Jewish scholars today.

Prof. Jiang wants us to read the Old Testament with the mind of Sigmund Freud and an understanding of the religious landscape 2500 years ago. Why is the concept of God's forgiveness for human sins so revolutionary compared to vengeance and obedience in Greek or Egyptian mythology? Because it allows us humans to disobey and then debate about it openly. Because God will forgive our mistakes on the journey of knowledge-seeking. The controversial thesis is: Obeying God or any authority—like kings, priests, or bureaucrats—blindly and without reasoning will kill the creativity of any society! And after that, it will also destroy that society.

The Bible has been co-opted long ago.
Prof. Jiang is good and inquisitive but he does not know.

1. The deity in the Old Testament is cruel and petty, even to his chosen, whom he Stockholm-syndromed.
2. The Egyptians knew the hebrews as the Hyksos, greedy foreign rulers in the lower Nile Delta that bowed to Set. Heebs were not enslaved, that was a lie.
3. Archaic sources seem to pin Yahweh as a canaanite god of war and storm.
4. At least on 2 accounts, Judeans were alleged as bowing to a golden donkey bust in the Temple.
5. Yhwh is not pronounced "Yahweh". It is simply pronounced without the vowels. Eee-*exhale-oo-*exhale, the noise that a donkey makes.

Yhwh is the egyptian god Set, a donkey-headed god of war (violence), deserts (desolation), and storms (calamity).
He aims to turn Earth into Mars, red with iron and lifeless. A deity of extinction.
The Greeks also called Set "iao-sabaoth", derived from hebrew "iah tsebha'oth", meaning "lord of the armies".
Here you can also pick up that it sounds close to "Yaldabaoth".
That's why ancient hebrews kept attacking their neighbours, as they do now. War is his favourite.

The True Omnipotence, upon seeing this imposter's transgressions, sent his son Christ to incarnate amongst the hebrew, into the body named Yeshua. His mission was 2-pronged:
- to save his people the hebrews from the inside (as they were instructed not to make merry with other tribes) via truth
- to pay off some blood oath foolishly sworn by the hebrews to Yhwh (the Word is the highest contract in the highest realms)

"Goy" used to mean "human", not "non-jew".
"the best of the gentiles", that's the Israelites. Yhwh is mocking his gullible chosen, that'll they be thrown right beside the rest of mankind once he has no more use of them.

The best deceiver tells 95% truth, and 5% untruth. That 5% strikes when it matters most.

It seems that our collective consciousness also revisits the same challenges—those we face today echo situations from 80 years ago, as well as patterns seen throughout the rise and fall of past empires.

In my view, the way forward requires a fundamental shift in how we make decisions. At present, fear shapes much of our collective behavior, and fear-driven actions inevitably create a distorted and unstable world. If we learn to act from a more open heart—choosing with empathy, clarity, and inner coherence—I believe a significant transformation of our collective consciousness is possible. >
> ​​@lilzenwi6714 Glad I could provide some illumination on the matter.  
>   
> I'm a Christian myself but I take the scholarship seriously too. From what I can tell the Old Testament is describing a real single prophetic tradition from Abraham to Christ.   
>   
> But the Bible was written with an emphasis on Monotheism and at this point the archeology reveals that what really happened wasn't so definitive the way the Bible makes it seem.  
>   
> From reading the Old Testament alone it's easy to get the impression that the Jewish religion was an island of monotheism in a sea of pagans.  
>   
> The reality is looking like (and the Bible shows this too once you can spot the remnants in Exodus) there was a Yahwey religion that didn't look too different from the other Pagan religions in the area. Then a monotheistic revolution happened.  
>   
> And there's evidence showing that the move towards monotheism didn't happen until after Alexander's conquest and Greek philosophy came to Israel.   
>   
> Which makes sense when you read Plato and realize how much the God Socrates is describing sounds like how we understand the concept today.  
>   
> Who knows though for sure. Humility of understanding goes a long way when studying this topic.

### **@halilekici617**
[View on YouTube](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=PlMNq44tBG0&lc=Ugx2OIKJJfqWMJZ2ei54AaABAg)

Professor,  the 2016 study  of "The Number of Variants in the Greek New Testament: A Proposed Estimate (pre-pub)" by Peter J Gurry analyses 5600 different Greek new testament manuscripts and finds that there are around 590000 variances in words between these manuscripts. This study is about new testament not Torah but in my opinion, it leads us to suspicion that we might not be reading the same bible as ancient Israeli people. Furthermore, Eve was not created from "rip bone" of Adam in Bible. There is a anatomic word for that which is "ala" and it is used in Daniel 7:5 as: "it had three ribs in its mouth between its teeth. It was told, ‘Get up and eat your fill of flesh!"  However, the word in Genesis which was translated as "rib bone" in  ancient Aramaic is "tsela" (şal-'öv) and that word is used to describe sides like in  arc of covenant or Solomon's temple. This situation propagates the idea that there is some meaning loss in translation from aramaic to greek to modern langauges as well.



My take from that is David might be a wise "Washington" or "Napoleon"   figure that defeats the enemies of the Israeli people and provider of the free reign of the Israeli state, instead of being a usurper. But any of this does not negate literary beauty and story telling of the modern Bible. Finally, I thank you kindly for sharing your insight in Youtube it feels like a fresh spring in arid internet environment.

Eating of the fruit (means taking part in) of the tree of knowledge of good and evil (judgement) does not mean simply becoming intelligent. This actually refers to the act of deciding right from wrong yourself rather than leaving that up to God (the divine logic of nature). After they began taking part in judgement, they began to see flaws in everything, even their own bodies. They no longer saw paradise. So, god didn’t reprimand them for putting on clothes and kick them out of Eden. They could no longer perceive it as Eden and they could no longer see the miraculous beauty in themselves. 
Adam, Eve, and the Serpent were spiritual essences rather than physical forms. Once the divine female took part in judgement, she and the divine male created a pattern of repeating ideas (judgments) onto and of themselves and universe. A repeating energetic idea takes on a form. But because spirit prevails, forms must eventually transform (die). Genesis describes the psychology of spirit becoming corporeal. It had to be Eve because the feminine has always been associated with physical matter (mater/mother). She is the gateway through which spirit becomes flesh. It is considered the original “sin” because, when we start on a path of spiritual understanding, we realize that the perpetual judgements in our own minds are a hindrance to clarity. In becoming enwrapped in judgment, Adam and Eve essentially created what Buddhists refer to as Maya, the illusion of this physical world.

[View on YouTube](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=PlMNq44tBG0&lc=UgxkZtJHnk8g5N3Gwt54AaABAg)

Is the concept of forgiveness in the Iliad different from the concept of forgiveness in the Bible? It sure sounds like it. 

In the Iliad, when Achilles and Priam share that scene, it is not reconciliation. It is two enemies seeing each other clearly for the first time. That moment is about understanding the grief of the other person. It is a human bridge, not a moral reset. No one’s guilt is lifted. No one changes sides. Nothing is erased. They sit in the same tent. They remain who they are. The war continues.

In the Bible, forgiveness tends to mean clearing the books. A wrong is absorbed. A relationship is repaired or renewed. The moral universe is reset. The person forgiven is restored to a righteous state. It has a spiritual charge.

### **@sputrain**

Thank you Professor, great presentation, I am a big fan and I appreciate your hard work. 

A few critical notes: 

1) There is also another explanation for the "death" referred to in the punishment for eating from the tree of life: losing immortality. The original humans can be interpreted as immortal since they were able to eat from the tree of life without restrictions. Once they eat from the tree of knowledge, they are banned from Eden and thus cannot eat from the tree of life. 

2) Bloom's theory that some passages of the Bible were written by women is a fringe minority view among biblical scholars. It is important to make this reservation when talking about subject this big. First of all, this debate is kind of redundant since we cannot know for sure, because the authors of Bible are anonymous. Secondly, it does not really make sense to me that a woman would be comissioned to write such an important text in a strictly patriarchal society of ancient Israel (e.g. Deuteronomy is radically patriarchal).

Here is a source :
According to one tradition, which attempts to explain why Eve was created while Adam was asleep:

"God wanted to create a helpmate for Adam. So God created the first Eve right in front of him. As Adam watched, the first Eve was created from the inside out—first her bones, then her flesh, and finally she was covered with skin. But when God offered her to Adam, he fled in disgust and hid in the Garden. So the first Eve was taken away, never to be heard from again."

(A common interpretation of this passage is that Adam was disgusted by the sight of the raw, bloody process of a creature being formed, which included seeing her "discharge and blood.")

Because Adam rejected this woman, God then decided to create the final wife, Eve, while Adam was in a deep sleep so he would not see the creation process and would accept the finished result, as recorded in Genesis 2:21-23 of the canonical Bible.

📜 What the Canonical Bible Says

The canonical Book of Genesis only presents two creation accounts of man and woman:

Genesis 1:27: God created man in His own image, "male and female He created them" (often interpreted as a simultaneous creation, which some traditions connect to Lilith).

Genesis 2:21-23: God created the woman, Eve, from Adam's rib while he was sleeping.
